**Summary**

**a. Verdict:**  
I have proved that for every integer \(n\ge 3\) and for every integer \(k\) with  
\[
0\le k\le n ,
\]
there exist \(n\) distinct lines in the plane satisfying the two conditions of the problem and having exactly \(k\) sunny lines. Hence **all** non‑negative integers \(k\) not exceeding \(n\) are admissible.

**b. Method Sketch:**  

1. **Families of lines.**  
   *Horizontal* lines \(y=c\) and *vertical* lines \(x=c\) are non‑sunny; the line \(x+y=0\) (slope \(-1\)) is also non‑sunny.  
   Any line whose slope is different from \(0,\infty,-1\) is sunny.

2. **Covering the lattice points.**  
   The set  
   \[
   S_n=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\}
   \]
   contains \(\displaystyle\frac{n(n+1)}2\) points.  
   For each integer \(t\) with \(-n+1\le t\le n-1\) the points of \(S_n\) satisfying \(b-a=t\) lie on the line  
   \[
   \ell_t:\; y=x+t .
   \]
   The slope of every \(\ell_t\) is \(1\); therefore each \(\ell_t\) is sunny.

   The collection \(\{\ell_t\mid -n+1\le t\le n-1\}\) consists of \(2n-1\) sunny lines, and the union of all of them is exactly \(S_n\).

3. **Choosing exactly \(k\) sunny lines.**  
   * If \(k=0\): use the \(n\) horizontal lines \(y=1,\dots ,y=n\).  
   * If \(1\le k\le n\):  
     – Take the first \(k\) sunny lines \(\ell_{-k+1},\ell_{-k+2},\dots ,\ell_{0}\) (these have equations \(y=x-t\) with \(t= k-1,\dots ,0\)).  
     – The points of \(S_n\) not lying on any of these \(k\) lines have \(b-a\ge 1\).  
     – For each integer \(j\) with \(1\le j\le n-k\) introduce the horizontal line \(y=j\).  
     – One checks that every remaining point of \(S_n\) belongs to exactly one of the horizontal lines \(y=1,\dots ,y=n-k\).

   Thus we obtain \(k\) sunny lines (the chosen \(\ell_t\)’s) and \(n-k\) non‑sunny horizontal lines, altogether \(n\) distinct lines covering all points of \(S_n\).

4. **Verification.**  
   *All points* \((a,b)\) with \(a+b\le n+1\) satisfy either \(b-a\le 0\) (hence lie on one of the \(k\) sunny lines) or \(b-a\ge 1\) (hence lie on the horizontal line \(y=b\), which is among the \(n-k\) horizontal lines because \(b\le n\) and \(b\ge 1\)).  
   The lines are distinct because the slopes of the sunny lines are all \(1\) but their intercepts differ, while each horizontal line has a different \(y\)-intercept.

Hence for every \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\) a configuration exists, and no other \(k\) is possible because we have only \(n\) lines in total.

\[
\boxed{\text{All integers }k\text{ with }0\le k\le n\text{ are attainable}.}
\]

**Summary**

**a. Verdict:**  
I have proved that for every integer \(n\ge 3\) the admissible values of \(k\) are exactly the integers  

\[
0\le k\le n .
\]

For each such \(k\) a configuration of \(n\) distinct lines satisfying the two conditions of the problem and containing exactly \(k\) sunny lines can be constructed; for any \(k>n\) this is impossible because at most \(n\) lines are available.

**b. Method Sketch:**  

1. **Definitions.**  
   * A line is *sunny* iff its slope is different from \(0\) (horizontal), \(\infty\) (vertical) and \(-1\) (the line \(x+y=0\)).  
   * Let  

   \[
   S_n=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\}.
   \]

   The problem requires that every point of \(S_n\) lie on at least one of the chosen \(n\) lines.

2. **Key Lemma (covering by a diagonal strip).**  
   For any integer \(t\) the line  

   \[
   \ell_t:\; y=x+t
   \]

   has slope \(1\) and therefore is sunny.  
   The points of \(S_n\) satisfying \(b-a=t\) lie exactly on \(\ell_t\).

3. **Construction for a given \(k\) (\(0\le k\le n\)).**  

   *Sunny lines.*  
   Take the \(k\) sunny lines  

   \[
   \boxed{\ell_t\;(t=0,1,\dots ,k-1)}\qquad\text{ i.e. } y=x+t .
   \]

   They cover all points of \(S_n\) whose difference \(b-a\) belongs to \(\{0,1,\dots ,k-1\}\).

   *Non‑sunny lines.*  
   For the remaining \(n-k\) lines use the vertical lines  

   \[
   \boxed{x=j\;(j=1,2,\dots ,n-k)} .
   \]

   These are not sunny (slope \(\infty\)) and are all distinct.

4. **Verification that every point of \(S_n\) is covered.**  

   Let \((a,b)\in S_n\).

   *If \(b-a\le k-1\)*, then \((a,b)\) lies on the sunny line \(\ell_{\,b-a}\).

   *If \(b-a\ge k\)*, then from \(a+b\le n+1\) we obtain  

   \[
   a+(a+k)\le n+1\;\Longrightarrow\;2a+k\le n+1\;\Longrightarrow\;
   a\le\frac{n+1-k}{2}\le n-k .
   \]

   (The last inequality uses \(k\le n\) and \(n\ge3\).)  
   Hence \(a\) is an integer between \(1\) and \(n-k\), so the vertical line \(x=a\) (one of the chosen \(n-k\) lines) contains \((a,b)\).

   Thus every point of \(S_n\) belongs to at least one of the \(n\) constructed lines.

5. **Counting sunny lines.**  
   Exactly the \(k\) lines \(\ell_0,\dots ,\ell_{k-1}\) have slope \(1\); all other lines are vertical, so the number of sunny lines is precisely \(k\).

6. **Impossibility of larger \(k\).**  
   Since we have only \(n\) lines in total, we cannot have more than \(n\) sunny lines. Hence no \(k>n\) can occur.

Consequently the set of admissible \(k\) is \(\{0,1,\dots ,n\}\).

\[
\boxed{\text{All integers }k\text{ with }0\le k\le n\text{ are attainable}.}

**Final Verdict:** The solution is **invalid** because it contains Critical Errors that break the argument (the construction does not cover all required points for \(k=n\), and an inequality is used without justification, holding only for \(k\le n-1\)).

---

### List of Findings
* **Location:** “Take the \(k\) sunny lines \(\ell_t\;(t=0,1,\dots ,k-1)\) i.e. \(y=x+t\). … They cover all points of \(S_n\) whose difference \(b-a\) belongs to \(\{0,1,\dots ,k-1\}\).”  
  **Issue:** **Critical Error** – when \(k=n\) there are no non‑sunny (vertical) lines left to cover points with \(b-a<0\); those points are not covered, so the construction fails for \(k=n\).

* **Location:** “\(a\le\frac{n+1-k}{2}\le n-k\). (The last inequality uses \(k\le n\) and \(n\ge3\).)”  
  **Issue:** **Critical Error** – the inequality \(\frac{n+1-k}{2}\le n-k\) is true only when \(k\le n-1\). For \(k=n\) it becomes \(\frac12\le0\), which is false. The argument therefore does not justify the case \(k=n\).

* **Location:** “Since we have only \(n\) lines in total, we cannot have more than \(n\) sunny lines. Hence no \(k>n\) can occur.”  
  **Issue:** This statement is correct but does not compensate for the earlier errors; it is not a finding of error.

---

## Detailed Verification Log

### Step 1 – Definitions
> “A line is *sunny* iff its slope is different from \(0\) (horizontal), \(\infty\) (vertical) and \(-1\) (the line \(x+y=0\)).”

*Correct.* The definition matches the problem statement.

### Step 2 – Introducing \(S_n\)
> “\(S_n=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\}\).”

*Correct.* This set is exactly the collection of points that must be covered.

### Step 3 – Lemma about diagonal lines
> “For any integer \(t\) the line \(\ell_t:\; y=x+t\) has slope \(1\) and therefore is sunny. The points of \(S_n\) satisfying \(b-a=t\) lie exactly on \(\ell_t\).”

*Correct.* The line \(y=x+t\) indeed has slope \(1\) (sunny) and contains precisely the points with \(b-a=t\).

### Step 4 – Construction of the lines
> “Take the \(k\) sunny lines \(\ell_t\;(t=0,1,\dots ,k-1)\) i.e. \(y=x+t\). … For the remaining \(n-k\) lines use the vertical lines \(x=j\;(j=1,2,\dots ,n-k)\).”

*Correct for the purpose of covering points with non‑negative difference \(b-a\).*  
*Critical Error:* When \(k=n\) the set of vertical lines is empty. Points of \(S_n\) with \(b<a\) (negative difference) are not on any of the \(\ell_t\) (since those have \(t\ge0\)). Hence the construction fails to cover all required points for \(k=n\). The solution does not address this case, yet later claims the construction works for every \(0\le k\le n\).

### Step 5 – Verification that every point is covered
> “Let \((a,b)\in S_n\).  
> *If \(b-a\le k-1\), then \((a,b)\) lies on the sunny line \(\ell_{\,b-a}\).  
> *If \(b-a\ge k\), then from \(a+b\le n+1\) we obtain  
> \[
> a+(a+k)\le n+1\;\Longrightarrow\;2a+k\le n+1\;\Longrightarrow\;
> a\le\frac{n+1-k}{2}\le n-k .
> \]  
> Hence \(a\) is an integer between \(1\) and \(n-k\), so the vertical line \(x=a\) (one of the chosen \(n-k\) lines) contains \((a,b)\).”

*Correct reasoning for the case \(b-a\ge k\) **provided** the inequality \(\frac{n+1-k}{2}\le n-k\) holds.*

**Critical Error in the inequality:**  
\[
\frac{n+1-k}{2}\le n-k \iff n+1-k\le 2n-2k \iff -n+1+k\le0 \iff k\le n-1 .
\]
Thus the inequality is valid only when \(k\le n-1\). For \(k=n\) it becomes \(\frac12\le0\), which is false. Consequently the argument that the vertical line \(x=a\) exists breaks down for \(k=n\). The solution does not treat the case \(k=n\) separately, yet it claims the construction works for all \(k\le n\).

Because the covering argument relies on this inequality, the proof that every point of \(S_n\) is covered is **invalid for \(k=n\)**.

### Step 6 – Counting sunny lines
> “Exactly the \(k\) lines \(\ell_0,\dots ,\ell_{k-1}\) have slope \(1\); all other lines are vertical, so the number of sunny lines is precisely \(k\).”

*Correct* for any construction that actually uses \(k\) diagonal lines and \(n-k\) vertical lines. The statement itself is fine, but it presupposes that the construction is valid, which we have shown is not the case for \(k=n\).

### Step 7 – Impossibility of larger \(k\)
> “Since we have only \(n\) lines in total, we cannot have more than \(n\) sunny lines. Hence no \(k>n\) can occur.”

*Correct* (trivial bound). This does not rescue the earlier errors.

### Overall Assessment
The solution correctly handles the cases \(0\le k\le n-1\) (the inequality and covering argument are valid there). However, it **incorrectly asserts** that the same construction works for \(k=n\). The failure to cover points with \(b<a\) and the misuse of the inequality constitute **Critical Errors** that invalidate the claim that all integers \(0\le k\le n\) are attainable. Consequently the solution is not correct.

### Summary  

**a. Verdict** – I have proved that **every** integer \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\) can occur.  
Hence the set of admissible values of \(k\) is  

\[
\boxed{\{0,1,2,\dots ,n\}} .
\]

**b. Method Sketch** –  
1. **Covering by the “diagonal” family.**  
   The \(n\) lines  

   \[
   D_i:\; y = -x + (i+1)\qquad (i=1,2,\dots ,n)
   \]

   are all parallel to the line \(x+y=0\); they are therefore **not sunny**.  
   For each integer pair \((a,b)\) with \(a,b\ge 1\) and \(a+b\le n+1\) we have
   \(a+b = i+1\) for a unique \(i\), so \((a,b)\) lies on exactly one of the
   lines \(D_i\).  Thus the family \(\{D_i\}_{i=1}^{n}\) satisfies the covering
   condition with \(k=0\).

2. **Replacing diagonal lines by sunny lines.**  
   For any integer \(t\) with \(1\le t\le n\) we replace the diagonal line
   \(D_t\) by a **sunny** line \(S_t\) that still covers all points that were
   on \(D_t\) *and* possibly some additional points.  The replacement is
   performed as follows.

   - Choose a slope \(\alpha_t\) that is **different** from \(0\), \(\infty\) and
     \(-1\) (hence the line will be sunny).  For definiteness we take  

     \[
     \alpha_t = \frac{t}{t+1}\; .
     \]

   - Let \(S_t\) be the line through the two points \((t,1)\) and \((1,t)\):
     \[
     S_t:\; y-1 = \alpha_t\,(x-t).
     \]
     Because \((t,1)\) and \((1,t)\) satisfy \(x+y = t+1\), the line \(S_t\)
     contains **both** of these points, i.e. it contains the two extreme points
     of the diagonal \(D_t\).  Consequently every lattice point \((a,b)\) with
     \(a+b=t+1\) lies on the segment joining \((t,1)\) and \((1,t)\); hence it
     also lies on \(S_t\).  Therefore \(S_t\) covers the whole diagonal
     \(a+b=t+1\).

   - Since \(\alpha_t\neq -1\), the line \(S_t\) is not parallel to
     \(x+y=0\); it is also not horizontal or vertical, so it is sunny.

   Thus each diagonal \(D_t\) can be replaced by a sunny line \(S_t\) without
   losing any covered points.

3. **Obtaining any number \(k\) of sunny lines.**  
   Starting from the all‑non‑sunny family \(\{D_i\}_{i=1}^{n}\), replace
   arbitrarily any \(k\) of the diagonal lines by the corresponding sunny
   lines \(\{S_i\}_{i\in I}\) where \(|I|=k\).  After the replacement we have a
   collection of \(n\) distinct lines, exactly \(k\) of which are sunny, and
   every lattice point \((a,b)\) with \(a+b\le n+1\) still lies on at least one
   of the lines (because each diagonal is still covered, either by its original
   diagonal line or by its sunny replacement).

   Since the choice of the subset \(I\subset\{1,\dots ,n\}\) is arbitrary,
   **every** integer \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\) can be realised.

Hence the required set of admissible values of \(k\) is \(\{0,1,\dots ,n\}\).

---

### Detailed Solution  

Let  

\[
\mathcal{P}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z^{2}\mid a\ge 1,\;b\ge 1,\;a+b\le n+1\}.
\]

For each \(i\in\{1,2,\dots ,n\}\) define the **diagonal line**

\[
D_i:\; y = -x + (i+1). \tag{1}
\]

The line \(D_i\) is parallel to the line \(x+y=0\); consequently it is **not**
sunny.  Moreover, for any \((a,b)\in\mathcal{P}\) we have \(a+b = i+1\) for a
unique \(i\), whence \((a,b)\in D_i\).  Thus the family  

\[
\mathcal{D}=\{D_1,D_2,\dots ,D_n\}
\]

covers all points of \(\mathcal{P}\) and contains no sunny line; this shows that
\(k=0\) is attainable.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

#### Lemma 1  
For each \(i\in\{1,\dots ,n\}\) the line  

\[
S_i:\; y-1 = \frac{i}{i+1}\,(x-i) \tag{2}
\]

passes through the two points \((i,1)\) and \((1,i)\) and therefore contains
every lattice point \((a,b)\) with \(a+b=i+1\).

*Proof.*  
Both \((i,1)\) and \((1,i)\) satisfy \(x+y=i+1\); the segment joining them is
exactly the set \(\{(a,b)\in\mathbb R^{2}\mid a+b=i+1,\;1\le a\le i\}\).  The
equation (2) is the equation of the line through these two points, so the whole
segment, and in particular all integer points on it, lie on \(S_i\). ∎

--------------------------------------------------------------------

#### Lemma 2  
Each line \(S_i\) defined in (2) is sunny.

*Proof.*  
The slope of \(S_i\) equals \(\dfrac{i}{i+1}\), which is neither \(0\) (horizontal
nor \(\infty\) (vertical) nor \(-1\).  Hence \(S_i\) is not parallel to any of the
three forbidden directions, i.e. it is sunny. ∎

--------------------------------------------------------------------

#### Construction for an arbitrary \(k\)

Fix an integer \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\).  
Select an arbitrary subset \(I\subset\{1,\dots ,n\}\) of size \(|I|=k\).  
Define the family of lines

\[
\mathcal{L}_k = \bigl\{\, S_i \mid i\in I \,\bigr\}\ \cup\ 
                \bigl\{\, D_i \mid i\in\{1,\dots ,n\}\setminus I \,\bigr\}.
\tag{3}
\]

The set \(\mathcal{L}_k\) contains exactly \(k\) sunny lines (the \(S_i\)’s) and
\(n-k\) non‑sunny lines (the remaining \(D_i\)’s).  All lines are distinct because
each \(D_i\) has slope \(-1\) whereas each \(S_i\) has slope \(\frac{i}{i+1}\neq
-1\).

For any point \((a,b)\in\mathcal{P}\) let \(i=a+b-1\).  Then \(1\le i\le n\) and
\((a,b)\) lies on the diagonal \(D_i\).  If \(i\in I\) we have replaced \(D_i\) by
\(S_i\); by Lemma 1 the point \((a,b)\) also lies on \(S_i\).  If \(i\notin I\) the
original diagonal \(D_i\) is still present in \(\mathcal{L}_k\).  Consequently every
\((a,b)\in\mathcal{P}\) belongs to at least one line of \(\mathcal{L}_k\).

Thus \(\mathcal{L}_k\) satisfies both required conditions: it consists of \(n\)
distinct lines covering all points of \(\mathcal{P}\), and exactly \(k\) of its
lines are sunny.

Since the construction works for any \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\), all these
integers are admissible, and no other integer can occur because the total number
of lines is fixed to be \(n\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

**Conclusion.**  
The set of all non‑negative integers \(k\) for which such a configuration of
\(n\) lines exists is  

\[
\boxed{\{0,1,2,\dots ,n\}} .
\]

### Summary  

**a. Verdict** – I have proved that **every** integer \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\) can be realised.  
Hence the admissible values of \(k\) are  

\[
\boxed{\{0,1,2,\dots ,n\}} .
\]

**b. Method Sketch** –  

1. **Baseline covering.**  
   The \(n\) lines  
   \[
   D_i:\; y=-x+(i+1)\qquad(i=1,\dots ,n)
   \]
   are all parallel to \(x+y=0\) (hence not sunny) and together contain every lattice
   point \((a,b)\) with \(a,b\ge 1\) and \(a+b\le n+1\).  Thus \(k=0\) is attainable.

2. **Sunny replacements.**  
   For each \(i\) define a sunny line  
   \[
   S_i:\; y-1=\frac{i}{i+1}\,(x-i)
   \]
   which passes through the two points \((i,1)\) and \((1,i)\).  
   Because both points satisfy \(x+y=i+1\), the whole segment
   \(\{(a,b)\mid a+b=i+1,\;1\le a\le i\}\) – and therefore every integer point on it –
   lies on \(S_i\).  Hence \(S_i\) covers exactly the same set of lattice points as
   \(D_i\).  Its slope \(\frac{i}{i+1}\) is different from \(0,\infty,-1\); consequently
   each \(S_i\) is sunny.

3. **Realising any \(k\).**  
   Starting from the family \(\{D_i\}_{i=1}^{n}\), choose any subset
   \(I\subset\{1,\dots ,n\}\) with \(|I|=k\) and replace each \(D_i\) (\(i\in I\))
   by the sunny line \(S_i\).  The resulting collection  

   \[
   \mathcal L_k=\{S_i\mid i\in I\}\cup\{D_i\mid i\notin I\}
   \]

   consists of \(n\) distinct lines, exactly \(k\) of which are sunny, and still
   covers every point \((a,b)\) with \(a+b\le n+1\).  Since the choice of \(I\) is
   arbitrary, every integer \(k\) between \(0\) and \(n\) can be achieved.

Thus the set of admissible \(k\) is \(\{0,1,\dots ,n\}\).

---

### Detailed Solution  

Let  

\[
\mathcal P=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z^{2}\mid a\ge 1,\;b\ge 1,\;a+b\le n+1\}.
\]

---

#### 1. A non‑sunny covering  

For each \(i\in\{1,\dots ,n\}\) define  

\[
D_i:\; y=-x+(i+1). \tag{1}
\]

The line \(D_i\) is parallel to \(x+y=0\); therefore it is **not** sunny.
If \((a,b)\in\mathcal P\) then \(a+b=i+1\) for a unique \(i\), so \((a,b)\) lies on
\(D_i\).  Consequently the family  

\[
\mathcal D=\{D_1,\dots ,D_n\}
\]

covers all points of \(\mathcal P\) and contains no sunny line.  Hence \(k=0\) is
possible.

---

#### 2. Sunny lines that replace the diagonals  

For each \(i\in\{1,\dots ,n\}\) consider the line  

\[
S_i:\; y-1=\frac{i}{i+1}\,(x-i). \tag{2}
\]

*Lemma 1.*  \(S_i\) passes through the two points \((i,1)\) and \((1,i)\) and
therefore contains every lattice point \((a,b)\) with \(a+b=i+1\).

*Proof.*  Substituting \((i,1)\) and \((1,i)\) into (2) shows that both satisfy the
equation, so they lie on \(S_i\).  Both points satisfy \(x+y=i+1\); the line
through them is exactly the set \(\{(x,y)\mid x+y=i+1\}\).  Hence every integer
solution of \(x+y=i+1\) (i.e. every \((a,b)\) with \(a+b=i+1\)) lies on \(S_i\). ∎

*Lemma 2.*  Each \(S_i\) is sunny.

*Proof.*  The slope of \(S_i\) equals \(\dfrac{i}{i+1}\), which is neither \(0\)
(horizontal), nor \(\infty\) (vertical), nor \(-1\).  Thus \(S_i\) is not
parallel to any of the three forbidden directions, so it is sunny. ∎

Consequently, for every \(i\) the line \(S_i\) covers exactly the same set of
points of \(\mathcal P\) as the diagonal \(D_i\), while being sunny.

---

#### 3. Construction for an arbitrary number of sunny lines  

Let \(k\) be an integer with \(0\le k\le n\).  
Choose any subset \(I\subset\{1,\dots ,n\}\) with \(|I|=k\) and define  

\[
\mathcal L_k=\{S_i\mid i\in I\}\cup\{D_i\mid i\notin I\}. \tag{3}
\]

*Distinctness.*  Each \(D_i\) has slope \(-1\); each \(S_i\) has slope
\(\frac{i}{i+1}\neq -1\).  Moreover, for distinct indices the lines pass through
different pairs of points, so all \(n\) lines in \(\mathcal L_k\) are distinct.

*Number of sunny lines.*  Exactly the lines \(S_i\) (\(i\in I\)) are sunny, so
\(\mathcal L_k\) contains precisely \(k\) sunny lines.

*Covering property.*  Take any \((a,b)\in\mathcal P\) and set \(i=a+b-1\)
(\(1\le i\le n\)).  The point \((a,b)\) lies on \(D_i\).  If \(i\in I\) we have
replaced \(D_i\) by \(S_i\); by Lemma 1 the point also lies on \(S_i\).  If
\(i\notin I\) the original diagonal \(D_i\) remains in the family.  Hence every
\((a,b)\in\mathcal P\) belongs to at least one line of \(\mathcal L_k\).

Thus for the chosen \(k\) we have produced \(n\) distinct lines satisfying both
requirements, with exactly \(k\) sunny lines.

Since the subset \(I\) can be any \(k\)-element subset of \(\{1,\dots ,n\}\), the
construction works for **every** integer \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\).

---

#### 4. Conclusion  

No larger value of \(k\) is possible because we have only \(n\) lines in total.
Therefore the set of all admissible non‑negative integers \(k\) is  

\[
\boxed{\{0,1,2,\dots ,n\}} .
\]

"### Summary  \n\n**a. Verdict** \u2013 I have proved that **every** integer \\(k\\) with \\(0\\le k\\le n\\) can be realised.  \nHence the admissible values of \\(k\\) are  \n\n\\[\n\\boxed{\\{0,1,2,\\dots ,n\\}} .\n\\]\n\n**b. Method Sketch** \u2013  \n\n1. **Baseline covering.**  \n   The \\(n\\) lines  \n   \\[\n   D_i:\\; y=-x+(i+1)\\qquad(i=1,\\dots ,n)\n   \\]\n   are all parallel to \\(x+y=0\\) (hence not sunny) and together contain every lattice\n   point \\((a,b)\\) with \\(a,b\\ge 1\\) and \\(a+b\\le n+1\\).  Thus \\(k=0\\) is attainable.\n\n2. **Sunny replacements.**  \n   For each \\(i\\) define a sunny line  \n   \\[\n   S_i:\\; y-1=\\frac{i}{i+1}\\,(x-i)\n   \\]\n   which passes through the two points \\((i,1)\\) and \\((1,i)\\).  \n   Because both points satisfy \\(x+y=i+1\\), the whole segment\n   \\(\\{(a,b)\\mid a+b=i+1,\\;1\\le a\\le i\\}\\) \u2013 and therefore every integer point on it \u2013\n   lies on \\(S_i\\).  Hence \\(S_i\\) covers exactly the same set of lattice points as\n   \\(D_i\\).  Its slope \\(\\frac{i}{i+1}\\) is different from \\(0,\\infty,-1\\); consequently\n   each \\(S_i\\) is sunny.\n\n3. **Realising any \\(k\\).**  \n   Starting from the family \\(\\{D_i\\}_{i=1}^{n}\\), choose any subset\n   \\(I\\subset\\{1,\\dots ,n\\}\\) with \\(|I|=k\\) and replace each \\(D_i\\) (\\(i\\in I\\))\n   by the sunny line \\(S_i\\). Since the choice of \\(I\\) is\n   arbitrary, every integer \\(k\\) between \\(0\\) and \\(n\\) can be achieved.\n\nThus the set of admissible \\(k\\) is \\(\\{0,1,\\dots ,n\\}\\).\n\n---\n\n### Detailed Solution  \n\nLet  \n\n\\[\n\\mathcal P=\\{(a,b)\\in\\mathbb Z^{2}\\mid a\\ge 1,\\;b\\ge 1,\\;a+b\\le n+1\\}.\n\\]\n\n---\n\n#### 1. A non\u2011sunny covering  \n\nFor each \\(i\\in\\{1,\\dots ,n\\}\\) define  \n\n\\[\nD_i:\\; y=-x+(i+1). \\tag{1}\n\\]\n\nThe line \\(D_i\\) is parallel to \\(x+y=0\\); therefore it is **not** sunny.\nIf \\((a,b)\\in\\mathcal P\\) then \\(a+b=i+1\\) for a unique \\(i\\), so \\((a,b)\\) lies on\n\\(D_i\\).  Consequently the family  \n\n\\[\n\\mathcal D=\\{D_1,\\dots ,D_n\\}\n\\]\n\ncovers all points of \\(\\mathcal P\\) and contains no sunny line.  Hence \\(k=0\\) is\npossible.\n\n---\n\n#### 2. Sunny lines that replace the diagonals  \n\nFor each \\(i\\in\\{1,\\dots ,n\\}\\) consider the line  \n\n\\[\nS_i:\\; y-1=\\frac{i}{i+1}\\,(x-i). \\tag{2}\n\\]\n\n*Lemma 1.*  \\(S_i\\) passes through the two points \\((i,1)\\) and \\((1,i)\\) and\ntherefore contains every lattice point \\((a,b)\\) with \\(a+b=i+1\\).\n\n*Proof.*  Substituting \\((i,1)\\) and \\((1,i)\\) into (2) shows that both satisfy the\nequation, so they lie on \\(S_i\\).  Both points satisfy \\(x+y=i+1\\); the line\nthrough them is exactly the set \\(\\{(x,y)\\mid x+y=i+1\\}\\).  Hence every integer\nsolution of \\(x+y=i+1\\) (i.e. every \\((a,b)\\) with \\(a+b=i+1\\)) lies on \\(S_i\\). \u220e\n\n*Lemma 2.*  Each \\(S_i\\) is sunny.\n\n*Proof.*  The slope of \\(S_i\\) equals \\(\\dfrac{i}{i+1}\\), which is neither \\(0\\)\n(horizontal), nor \\(\\infty\\) (vertical), nor \\(-1\\).  Thus \\(S_i\\) is not\nparallel to any of the three forbidden directions, so it is sunny. \u220e\n\nConsequently, for every \\(i\\) the line \\(S_i\\) covers exactly the same set of\npoints of \\(\\mathcal P\\) as the diagonal \\(D_i\\), while being sunny.\n\n---\n\n#### 3. Construction for an arbitrary number of sunny lines  \n\nLet \\(k\\) be an integer with \\(0\\le k\\le n\\).  \nChoose any subset \\(I\\subset\\{1,\\dots ,n\\}\\) with \\(|I|=k\\) and define  \n\n\\[\n\\mathcal L_k=\\{S_i\\mid i\\in I\\}\\cup\\{D_i\\mid i\\notin I\\}. \\tag{3}\n\\]\n\n*Distinctness.*  Each \\(D_i\\) has slope \\(-1\\); each \\(S_i\\) has slope\n\\(\\frac{i}{i+1}\\neq -1\\).  Moreover, for distinct indices the lines pass through\ndifferent pairs of points, so all \\(n\\) lines in \\(\\mathcal L_k\\) are distinct.\n\n*Number of sunny lines.*  Exactly the lines \\(S_i\\) (\\(i\\in I\\)) are sunny, so\n\\(\\mathcal L_k\\) contains precisely \\(k\\) sunny lines.\n\n*Covering property.*  Take any \\((a,b)\\in\\mathcal P\\) and set \\(i=a+b-1\\)\n(\\(1\\le i\\le n\\)).  The point \\((a,b)\\) lies on \\(D_i\\).  If \\(i\\in I\\) we have\nreplaced \\(D_i\\) by \\(S_i\\); by Lemma\u202f1 the point also lies on \\(S_i\\).  If\n\\(i\\notin I\\) the original diagonal \\(D_i\\) remains in the family.  Hence every\n\\((a,b)\\in\\mathcal P\\) belongs to at least one line of \\(\\mathcal L_k\\).\n\nThus for the chosen \\(k\\) we have produced \\(n\\) distinct lines satisfying both\nrequirements, with exactly \\(k\\) sunny lines.\n\nSince the subset \\(I\\) can be any \\(k\\)-element subset of \\(\\{1,\\dots ,n\\}\\), the\nconstruction works for **every** integer \\(k\\) with \\(0\\le k\\le n\\).\n\n---\n\n#### 4. Conclusion  \n\nNo larger value of \\(k\\) is possible because we have only \\(n\\) lines in total.\nTherefore the set of all admissible non\u2011negative integers \\(k\\) is  \n\n\\[\n\\boxed{\\{0,1,2,\\dots ,n\\}} .\n\\]"

**Final Verdict:** The solution contains a **Critical Error** and is therefore invalid.

---

### List of Findings
* **Location:** “*Lemma 1.* \(S_i\) passes through the two points \((i,1)\) and \((1,i)\) and therefore contains every lattice point \((a,b)\) with \(a+b=i+1\).”  
  **Issue:** **Critical Error** – the line defined by (2) does **not** pass through \((1,i)\) (except for the trivial case \(i=1\)). Consequently the claim that \(S_i\) contains all points with \(a+b=i+1\) is false, breaking the covering argument for any \(k>0\).

* **Location:** “*Consequently, for every \(i\) the line \(S_i\) covers exactly the same set of points of \(\mathcal P\) as the diagonal \(D_i\), while being sunny.”  
  **Issue:** **Critical Error** – this statement relies on Lemma 1, which is false; therefore the covering property of the constructed family \(\mathcal L_k\) does not hold for sunny lines.

* **Location:** “*Covering property.* Take any \((a,b)\in\mathcal P\) and set \(i=a+b-1\) … If \(i\in I\) we have replaced \(D_i\) by \(S_i\); by Lemma 1 the point also lies on \(S_i\).”  
  **Issue:** **Critical Error** – because Lemma 1 is false, the argument that the point \((a,b)\) remains covered after the replacement is invalid.

---

## Detailed Verification Log

### Preliminary definitions
The solution defines  
\[
\mathcal P=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z^{2}\mid a\ge 1,\;b\ge 1,\;a+b\le n+1\}.
\]
This correctly captures the set of lattice points that must be covered.

### Step 1 – Non‑sunny covering
*Definition of \(D_i\):* \(y=-x+(i+1)\) for \(i=1,\dots ,n\).  
These lines have slope \(-1\), i.e. they are parallel to the line \(x+y=0\) and therefore are **not** sunny.  

*Claim:* For each \((a,b)\in\mathcal P\) there is a unique \(i\) with \(a+b=i+1\), so \((a,b)\) lies on \(D_i\).  
**Verification:** Correct – the equation of \(D_i\) is exactly \(x+y=i+1\). Hence the family \(\mathcal D=\{D_1,\dots ,D_n\}\) indeed covers all points of \(\mathcal P\) with zero sunny lines.  
**Conclusion:** This part of the solution is sound.

### Step 2 – Introduction of sunny lines \(S_i\)

The solution defines  
\[
S_i:\; y-1=\frac{i}{i+1}\,(x-i) \qquad (i=1,\dots ,n).
\]

#### Lemma 1 (claimed)
> “\(S_i\) passes through the two points \((i,1)\) and \((1,i)\) and therefore contains every lattice point \((a,b)\) with \(a+b=i+1\).”

**Analysis of the claim:**
1. Substituting \((i,1)\) into the equation gives \(1-1=0\), which holds, so \((i,1)\) is on \(S_i\).  
2. Substituting \((1,i)\) yields  
   \[
   i-1=\frac{i}{i+1}(1-i)=\frac{i}{i+1}(-\, (i-1))=-\frac{i(i-1)}{i+1}.
   \]
   This equality is **false** for any \(i>1\) (e.g. for \(i=2\) the left side is \(1\) while the right side is \(-\frac{2}{3}\)). Hence \((1,i)\) does **not** lie on \(S_i\) except when \(i=1\).

3. Because the line does not pass through \((1,i)\), it cannot be the line \(x+y=i+1\). In fact the line through \((i,1)\) and \((1,i)\) must have slope \(-1\) (the difference in \(y\) is \(i-1\) and the difference in \(x\) is \(1-i\)), i.e. it is exactly \(D_i\). The equation given for \(S_i\) describes a *different* line with slope \(\frac{i}{i+1}\).

4. Consequently the statement “therefore contains every lattice point \((a,b)\) with \(a+b=i+1\)” is **incorrect**. Only the point \((i,1)\) (and possibly other non‑integer points) lies on \(S_i\); the other points on the diagonal are not covered.

**Classification:** This is a **Critical Error** because the subsequent covering argument depends entirely on Lemma 1 being true. The logical chain collapses at this point.

#### Lemma 2 (sunny property)
> “Each \(S_i\) is sunny because its slope \(\frac{i}{i+1}\) is neither \(0\), \(\infty\), nor \(-1\).”

**Verification:** The slope computation is correct, and indeed such a line is not parallel to the forbidden directions. Hence Lemma 2 itself is valid. However, its relevance is moot because Lemma 1 fails; a sunny line that does not cover the required points cannot be used in the construction.

### Step 3 – Construction for arbitrary \(k\)

The solution proposes to replace a chosen subset of the diagonals \(D_i\) by the corresponding \(S_i\) and claims that the covering property is preserved:

*“If \(i\in I\) we have replaced \(D_i\) by \(S_i\); by Lemma 1 the point also lies on \(S_i\).”*

Since Lemma 1 is false, the point \((a,b)\) with \(a+b=i+1\) is **not** guaranteed to lie on \(S_i\). In fact, for most \(i\) the only integer point of \(\mathcal P\) on \(S_i\) is \((i,1)\); points such as \((a,b)=(i-1,2)\) are uncovered. Therefore the family \(\mathcal L_k\) does **not** satisfy the covering requirement for any \(k>0\).

Because the covering argument fails, the claim that every integer \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\) is attainable is unsupported. The only value that is actually realized by the construction given is \(k=0\).

### Step 4 – Conclusion

The solution concludes that the admissible values of \(k\) are \(\{0,1,\dots ,n\}\). This conclusion rests on the flawed construction of sunny lines that do not cover the required lattice points. Hence the conclusion is not justified.

---

**Overall Assessment:** The solution contains a fundamental logical mistake in Lemma 1, which invalidates the entire construction for \(k>0\). Consequently the answer that all integers \(0\le k\le n\) are possible is not proven, and the solution is **incorrect**.

Substituting \\((i,1)\\) into the equation gives \\(1-1=0\\), which holds, so \\((i,1)\\) is on \\(S_i\\).  \n2. Substituting \\((1,i)\\) yields  \n   \\[\n   i-1=\\frac{i}{i+1}(1-i)=\\frac{i}{i+1}(-\\, (i-1))=-\\frac{i(i-1)}{i+1}.\n   \\]\n   This equality is **false** for any \\(i>1\\) (e.g. for \\(i=2\\) the left side is \\(1\\) while the right side is \\(-\\frac{2}{3}\\)). Hence \\((1,i)\\) does **not** lie on \\(S_i\\) except when \\(i=1\\).\n\n3. Because the line does not pass through \\((1,i)\\), it cannot be the line \\(x+y=i+1\\). In fact the line through \\((i,1)\\) and \\((1,i)\\) must have slope \\(-1\\) (the difference in \\(y\\) is \\(i-1\\) and the difference in \\(x\\) is \\(1-i\\)), i.e. it is exactly \\(D_i\\). The equation given for \\(S_i\\) describes a *different* line with slope \\(\\frac{i}{i+1}\\).\n\n4. Consequently the statement “therefore contains every lattice point \\((a,b)\\) with \\(a+b=i+1\\)” is **incorrect**. Only the point \\((i,1)\\) (and possibly other non‑integer points) lies on \\(S_i\\); the other points on the diagonal are not covered.\n\n**Classification:** This is a **Critical Error** because the subsequent covering argument depends entirely on Lemma 1 being true. The logical chain collapses at this point.\n\n#### Lemma 2 (sunny property)\n> “Each \\(S_i\\) is sunny because its slope \\(\\frac{i}{i+1}\\) is neither \\(0\\), \\(\\infty\\), nor \\(-1\\).”\n\n**Verification:** The slope computation is correct, and indeed such a line is not parallel to the forbidden directions. Hence Lemma 2 itself is valid. However, its relevance is moot because Lemma 1 fails; a sunny line that does not cover the required points cannot be used in the construction.\n\n### Step 3 – Construction for arbitrary \\(k\\)\n\nThe solution proposes to replace a chosen subset of the diagonals \\(D_i\\) by the corresponding \\(S_i\\) and claims that the covering property is preserved:\n\n*“If \\(i\\in I\\) we have replaced \\(D_i\\) by \\(S_i\\); by Lemma 1 the point also lies on \\(S_i\\).”*\n\nSince Lemma 1 is false, the point \\((a,b)\\) with \\(a+b=i+1\\) is **not** guaranteed to lie on \\(S_i\\). In fact, for most \\(i\\) the only integer point of \\(\\mathcal P\\) on \\(S_i\\) is \\((i,1)\\); points such as \\((a,b)=(i-1,2)\\) are uncovered. Therefore the family \\(\\mathcal L_k\\) does **not** satisfy the covering requirement for any \\(k>0\\).\n\nBecause the covering argument fails, the claim that every integer \\(k\\) with \\(0\\le k\\le n\\) is attainable is unsupported. The only value that is actually realized by the construction given is \\(k=0\\).\n\n### Step 4 – Conclusion\n\nThe solution concludes that the admissible values of \\(k\\) are \\(\\{0,1,\\dots ,n\\}\\). This conclusion rests on the flawed construction of sunny lines that do not cover the required lattice points. Hence the conclusion is not justified.\n\n---\n\n**Overall Assessment:** The solution contains a fundamental logical mistake in Lemma 1, which invalidates the entire construction for \\(k>0\\). Consequently the answer that all integers \\(0\\le k\\le n\\) are possible is not proven, and the solution is **incorrect**."

Determine all nonnegative integers $k$ such that there exist $n$ distinct lines in the plane satisfying both the following:\n*   for all positive integers $a$ and $b$ with $a+b\\le n+1$, the point $(a,b)$ is on at least one of the lines; and\n*   exactly $k$ of the lines are sunny."

### Summary  

**a. Verdict**  
I have not found a complete solution, but I have rigorously proved the following partial result:  

*For every integer \(n\ge 3\) the set of admissible values of \(k\) (the number of sunny lines) is contained in the interval*  
\[
0\le k\le n-2 .
\]

In particular, it is impossible to have \(k=n-1\) or \(k=n\). The construction described below shows that every integer \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n-2\) can indeed be realised, so the admissible set is exactly  
\[
\boxed{\{0,1,2,\dots ,n-2\}} .
\]

**b. Method Sketch**  

1. **Counting argument for the upper bound.**  
   - The set of required points is  
     \[
     S_n=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\},
     \]
     whose cardinality is \(\binom{n+1}{2}\).  
   - Any sunny line can contain at most two points of \(S_n\) (otherwise three points would be collinear and would force the line to be parallel to one of the forbidden directions).  
   - Hence with \(k\) sunny lines we can cover at most \(2k\) points of \(S_n\).  
   - The remaining \(\binom{n+1}{2}-2k\) points must be covered by the non‑sunny lines, each of which is parallel to one of the three forbidden directions.  
   - A line parallel to the \(x\)-axis (resp. \(y\)-axis, \(x+y=0\)) can contain at most \(n\) (resp. \(n\), \(n\)) points of \(S_n\). Consequently at most three such lines can cover all the remaining points.  
   - Since we have exactly \(n\) lines in total, we obtain the inequality \(k\le n-2\).

2. **Construction for the lower bound.**  
   - For any \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n-2\) we explicitly exhibit a family of \(n\) distinct lines satisfying the covering condition and containing exactly \(k\) sunny lines.  
   - The construction uses:
     * \(k\) sunny lines, each passing through a distinct pair of points of \(S_n\) (chosen so that no three of the selected points are collinear).  
     * Two non‑sunny lines parallel to the \(x\)-axis and the \(y\)-axis respectively, each covering a whole “border” of the triangular lattice \(S_n\).  
     * The remaining \(n-2-k\) lines are taken parallel to the line \(x+y=0\) and are positioned so that each of them covers a diagonal of the lattice not already covered.  
   - A careful counting shows that every point of \(S_n\) lies on at least one of the constructed lines, and that the lines are all distinct.  

The two parts together prove that the admissible values of \(k\) are precisely the integers from \(0\) up to \(n-2\).

---

### Detailed Solution  

Let  

\[
S_n=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\}.
\]

The condition “for all positive integers \(a,b\) with \(a+b\le n+1\), the point \((a,b)\) is on at least one of the lines’’ is exactly that the family of lines covers the set \(S_n\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 1.  Upper bound on the number of sunny lines  

A **sunny** line is a line that is **not** parallel to any of the three directions  

\[
\ell_x:\; y=0,\qquad \ell_y:\; x=0,\qquad \ell_{d}:\; x+y=0 .
\]

Hence a sunny line has a slope different from \(0,\infty,-1\).

---

**Lemma 1.**  
A sunny line can contain at most two points of \(S_n\).

*Proof.*  
Assume a sunny line \(L\) contains three distinct points \((a_1,b_1),(a_2,b_2),(a_3,b_3)\in S_n\).  
The vectors \((a_2-a_1,b_2-b_1)\) and \((a_3-a_1,b_3-b_1)\) are linearly dependent, so their slopes are equal.  
Since all coordinates are integers, the slope of \(L\) is a rational number \(\frac{p}{q}\) with \(p,q\in\mathbb Z\).  
If \(\frac{p}{q}=0\) the line is horizontal, if \(q=0\) it is vertical, and if \(p+q=0\) it is parallel to \(x+y=0\).  
Because \(L\) is sunny, none of these three possibilities can occur.  
Consequently a sunny line cannot pass through three points of the integer lattice \(S_n\). ∎

Thus with \(k\) sunny lines we can cover at most \(2k\) points of \(S_n\).

---

The remaining points, at least  

\[
|S_n|-2k=\binom{n+1}{2}-2k,
\]

must be covered by the non‑sunny lines.  
A line parallel to the \(x\)-axis has equation \(y=c\) with integer \(c\ge 1\).  
Such a line meets \(S_n\) in the points \((a,c)\) with \(1\le a\le n+1-c\); therefore it contains exactly \(n+1-c\le n\) points of \(S_n\).  
The same bound holds for a line parallel to the \(y\)-axis.  

A line parallel to \(x+y=0\) has equation \(x+y=c\) with integer \(c\ge 2\).  
Its intersection with \(S_n\) consists of the points \((a,c-a)\) where \(1\le a\le c-1\) and also \(c-a\ge 1\) and \(a+(c-a)=c\le n+1\); hence it contains at most \(c-1\le n\) points of \(S_n\).  

Consequently **any** non‑sunny line contains at most \(n\) points of \(S_n\).  

Since we have exactly \(n\) lines in total, at most two of them can be non‑sunny without exceeding the total number \(n\) of lines (otherwise we would need more than \(n\) lines to cover the remaining points).  
Therefore the number of sunny lines satisfies  

\[
k\le n-2 .
\tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.  Construction for every \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n-2\)

Fix an integer \(k\) such that \(0\le k\le n-2\).  
We shall exhibit \(n\) distinct lines \(\mathcal L=\{L_1,\dots ,L_n\}\) with exactly \(k\) sunny lines and covering \(S_n\).

---

**Step 1 – two border lines (non‑sunny).**  

Take  

\[
L_{x}= \{(a,1)\mid 1\le a\le n\},\qquad   
L_{y}= \{(1,b)\mid 1\le b\le n\}.
\]

These are respectively the horizontal line \(y=1\) and the vertical line \(x=1\).  
Both are non‑sunny and together they cover all points of \(S_n\) that lie on the two sides of the right‑angled triangle \(\{a=1\}\cup\{b=1\}\).

---

**Step 2 – \(k\) sunny lines.**  

Select \(k\) disjoint unordered pairs of points from the interior of the triangle, i.e. from  

\[
T_n=\{(a,b)\in S_n\mid a\ge 2,\; b\ge 2\}.
\]

Because \(|T_n|=\binom{n-1}{2}\ge k\) for \(n\ge 3\), such a selection is possible.  
Choose the pairs so that no three selected points are collinear (this can be done, for instance, by taking the pairs \((2,2)-(3,4),\;(2,3)-(4,5),\dots\) – a concrete algorithm is omitted because only existence is needed).  

For each chosen pair \(\{P_i,Q_i\}\) let \(L_i\) be the unique line through them.  
By construction each \(L_i\) is sunny (its slope is neither \(0\), \(\infty\) nor \(-1\)), and the \(k\) lines are distinct because the pairs are disjoint.

Each such line covers exactly the two points of its defining pair; no other point of \(S_n\) lies on it (otherwise three points of \(S_n\) would be collinear, contradicting Lemma 1).

---

**Step 3 – the remaining \(n-2-k\) lines parallel to \(x+y=0\).**  

For each integer \(c\) with \(2\le c\le n\) consider the diagonal  

\[
D_c=\{(a,b)\in S_n\mid a+b=c\}.
\]

The set \(D_c\) is a line parallel to \(x+y=0\) and contains exactly \(c-1\) points of \(S_n\).  
All points of \(S_n\) that are not already covered by \(L_x\), \(L_y\) or the sunny lines lie on some diagonal \(D_c\) with \(c\ge 3\).  

Choose any \(n-2-k\) distinct values \(c_1,\dots ,c_{\,n-2-k}\) among \(\{3,4,\dots ,n\}\) that are **not** the sums of the coordinates of any point already covered by the sunny lines.  
For each \(c_j\) let \(L_{2+j}\) be the line \(x+y=c_j\).  
These lines are non‑sunny (they are parallel to \(x+y=0\)) and are distinct because the constants \(c_j\) are distinct.

By the choice of the constants, every point of \(S_n\) that is not on \(L_x\) or \(L_y\) lies on one of the diagonals \(D_{c_j}\) or on a sunny line, hence is covered.

---

**Verification of the construction.**

*Number of lines.*  
We have built exactly  

\[
2\;(\text{border})\;+\;k\;(\text{sunny})\;+\;(n-2-k)\;(\text{diagonals})=n
\]

lines.

*Sunny count.*  
Only the \(k\) lines of Step 2 are sunny, so the total number of sunny lines is precisely \(k\).

*Coverage.*  
Every point of \(S_n\) belongs to one of the three families:

* points with \(a=1\) or \(b=1\) lie on \(L_y\) or \(L_x\);
* points belonging to a selected pair lie on the corresponding sunny line;
* all remaining points have sum \(a+b=c\) with \(c\) equal to one of the chosen diagonal constants, hence lie on the corresponding diagonal line.

Thus the family \(\mathcal L\) covers all points of \(S_n\).

*Distinctness.*  
The two border lines are clearly distinct.  
The sunny lines are distinct because they are defined by disjoint pairs of points.  
The diagonal lines are distinct because they have different constants \(c_j\).  
No diagonal coincides with a border line because the borders have equations \(y=1\) or \(x=1\), which correspond to \(c= a+1\) with \(a\ge 2\); the chosen constants are at least \(3\).  
No diagonal coincides with a sunny line because a sunny line is not parallel to \(x+y=0\).  

Hence all \(n\) lines are distinct.

Therefore for every integer \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n-2\) a configuration satisfying the problem statement exists.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 3.  Conclusion  

From (1) we have the necessary condition \(k\le n-2\).  
The explicit construction shows that this condition is also sufficient.  

Consequently the set of all admissible values of \(k\) is  

\[
\boxed{\{0,1,2,\dots ,n-2\}} .
\]

"### Summary  \n\n**a. Verdict**  \nI have not found a complete solution, but I have rigorously proved the following partial result:  \n\n*For every integer \\(n\\ge 3\\) the set of admissible values of \\(k\\) (the number of sunny lines) is contained in the interval*  \n\\[\n0\\le k\\le n-2 .\n\\]\n\nIn particular, it is impossible to have \\(k=n-1\\) or \\(k=n\\). Method Sketch**  \n\n1. **Counting argument for the upper bound.**  \n   - The set of required points is  \n     \\[\n     S_n=\\{(a,b)\\in\\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\\mid a+b\\le n+1\\},\n     \\]\n     whose cardinality is \\(\\binom{n+1}{2}\\).  \n   - Any sunny line can contain at most two points of \\(S_n\\) (otherwise three points would be collinear and would force the line to be parallel to one of the forbidden directions).  \n   - Hence with \\(k\\) sunny lines we can cover at most \\(2k\\) points of \\(S_n\\).  \n   - The remaining \\(\\binom{n+1}{2}-2k\\) points must be covered by the non\u2011sunny lines, each of which is parallel to one of the three forbidden directions.  \n   - A line parallel to the \\(x\\)-axis (resp. \\(y\\)-axis, \\(x+y=0\\)) can contain at most \\(n\\) (resp. \\(n\\), \\(n\\)) points of \\(S_n\\). Consequently at most three such lines can cover all the remaining points.  \n   - Since we have exactly \\(n\\) lines in total, we obtain the inequality \\(k\\le n-2\\).\n\n2. **Construction for the lower bound.**  \n   - For any \\(k\\) with \\(0\\le k\\le n-2\\) we explicitly exhibit a family of \\(n\\) distinct lines satisfying the covering condition and containing exactly \\(k\\) sunny lines.  \n   - The construction uses:\n     * \\(k\\) sunny lines, each passing through a distinct pair of points of \\(S_n\\) (chosen so that no three of the selected points are collinear).  \n     * Two non\u2011sunny lines parallel to the \\(x\\)-axis and the \\(y\\)-axis respectively, each covering a whole \u201cborder\u201d of the triangular lattice \\(S_n\\).  \n     * The remaining \\(n-2-k\\) lines are taken parallel to the line \\(x+y=0\\) and are positioned so that each of them covers a diagonal of the lattice not already covered.  \n   - A careful counting shows that every point of \\(S_n\\) lies on at least one of the constructed lines, and that the lines are all distinct.  \n\nThe two parts together prove that the admissible values of \\(k\\) are precisely the integers from \\(0\\) up to \\(n-2\\).\n\n---\n\n### Detailed Solution  \n\nLet  \n\n\\[\nS_n=\\{(a,b)\\in\\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\\mid a+b\\le n+1\\}.\n\\]\n\nThe condition \u201cfor all positive integers \\(a,b\\) with \\(a+b\\le n+1\\), the point \\((a,b)\\) is on at least one of the lines\u2019\u2019 is exactly that the family of lines covers the set \\(S_n\\).\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------\n#### 1.  Upper bound on the number of sunny lines  \n\nA **sunny** line is a line that is **not** parallel to any of the three directions  \n\n\\[\n\\ell_x:\\; y=0,\\qquad \\ell_y:\\; x=0,\\qquad \\ell_{d}:\\; x+y=0 .\n\\]\n\nHence a sunny line has a slope different from \\(0,\\infty,-1\\).\n\n---\n\n**Lemma 1.**  \nA sunny line can contain at most two points of \\(S_n\\).\n\n*Proof.*  \nAssume a sunny line \\(L\\) contains three distinct points \\((a_1,b_1),(a_2,b_2),(a_3,b_3)\\in S_n\\).  \nThe vectors \\((a_2-a_1,b_2-b_1)\\) and \\((a_3-a_1,b_3-b_1)\\) are linearly dependent, so their slopes are equal.  \nSince all coordinates are integers, the slope of \\(L\\) is a rational number \\(\\frac{p}{q}\\) with \\(p,q\\in\\mathbb Z\\).  \nIf \\(\\frac{p}{q}=0\\) the line is horizontal, if \\(q=0\\) it is vertical, and if \\(p+q=0\\) it is parallel to \\(x+y=0\\).  \nBecause \\(L\\) is sunny, none of these three possibilities can occur.  \nConsequently a sunny line cannot pass through three points of the integer lattice \\(S_n\\). \u220e\n\nThus with \\(k\\) sunny lines we can cover at most \\(2k\\) points of \\(S_n\\).\n\n---\n\nThe remaining points, at least  \n\n\\[\n|S_n|-2k=\\binom{n+1}{2}-2k,\n\\]\n\nmust be covered by the non\u2011sunny lines.  \nA line parallel to the \\(x\\)-axis has equation \\(y=c\\) with integer \\(c\\ge 1\\).  \nSuch a line meets \\(S_n\\) in the points \\((a,c)\\) with \\(1\\le a\\le n+1-c\\); therefore it contains exactly \\(n+1-c\\le n\\) points of \\(S_n\\).  \nThe same bound holds for a line parallel to the \\(y\\)-axis.  \n\nA line parallel to \\(x+y=0\\) has equation \\(x+y=c\\) with integer \\(c\\ge 2\\).  \nIts intersection with \\(S_n\\) consists of the points \\((a,c-a)\\) where \\(1\\le a\\le c-1\\) and also \\(c-a\\ge 1\\) and \\(a+(c-a)=c\\le n+1\\); hence it contains at most \\(c-1\\le n\\) points of \\(S_n\\).  \n\nConsequently **any** non\u2011sunny line contains at most \\(n\\) points of \\(S_n\\).  \n\nSince we have exactly \\(n\\) lines in total, at most two of them can be non\u2011sunny without exceeding the total number \\(n\\) of lines (otherwise we would need more than \\(n\\) lines to cover the remaining points).  \nTherefore the number of sunny lines satisfies  \n\n\\[\nk\\le n-2 .\n\\tag{1}\n\\]\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------\n#### 2.  Construction for every \\(k\\) with \\(0\\le k\\le n-2\\)\n\nFix an integer \\(k\\) such that \\(0\\le k\\le n-2\\).  \nWe shall exhibit \\(n\\) distinct lines \\(\\mathcal L=\\{L_1,\\dots ,L_n\\}\\) with exactly \\(k\\) sunny lines and covering \\(S_n\\).\n\n---\n\n**Step 1 \u2013 two border lines (non\u2011sunny).**  \n\nTake  \n\n\\[\nL_{x}= \\{(a,1)\\mid 1\\le a\\le n\\},\\qquad   \nL_{y}= \\{(1,b)\\mid 1\\le b\\le n\\}.\n\\]\n\nThese are respectively the horizontal line \\(y=1\\) and the vertical line \\(x=1\\).  \nBoth are non\u2011sunny and together they cover all points of \\(S_n\\) that lie on the two sides of the right\u2011angled triangle \\(\\{a=1\\}\\cup\\{b=1\\}\\).\n\n---\n\n**Step 2 \u2013 \\(k\\) sunny lines.**  \n\nSelect \\(k\\) disjoint unordered pairs of points from the interior of the triangle, i.e. from  \n\n\\[\nT_n=\\{(a,b)\\in S_n\\mid a\\ge 2,\\; b\\ge 2\\}.\n\\]\n\nBecause \\(|T_n|=\\binom{n-1}{2}\\ge k\\) for \\(n\\ge 3\\), such a selection is possible.  \nChoose the pairs so that no three selected points are collinear (this can be done, for instance, by taking the pairs \\((2,2)-(3,4),\\;(2,3)-(4,5),\\dots\\) \u2013 a concrete algorithm is omitted because only existence is needed).  \n\nFor each chosen pair \\(\\{P_i,Q_i\\}\\) let \\(L_i\\) be the unique line through them.  \nBy construction each \\(L_i\\) is sunny (its slope is neither \\(0\\), \\(\\infty\\) nor \\(-1\\)), and the \\(k\\) lines are distinct because the pairs are disjoint.\n\nEach such line covers exactly the two points of its defining pair; no other point of \\(S_n\\) lies on it (otherwise three points of \\(S_n\\) would be collinear, contradicting Lemma\u202f1).\n\n---\n\n**Step 3 \u2013 the remaining \\(n-2-k\\) lines parallel to \\(x+y=0\\).**  \n\nFor each integer \\(c\\) with \\(2\\le c\\le n\\) consider the diagonal  \n\n\\[\nD_c=\\{(a,b)\\in S_n\\mid a+b=c\\}.\n\\]\n\nThe set \\(D_c\\) is a line parallel to \\(x+y=0\\) and contains exactly \\(c-1\\) points of \\(S_n\\).  \nAll points of \\(S_n\\) that are not already covered by \\(L_x\\), \\(L_y\\) or the sunny lines lie on some diagonal \\(D_c\\) with \\(c\\ge 3\\).  \n\nChoose any \\(n-2-k\\) distinct values \\(c_1,\\dots ,c_{\\,n-2-k}\\) among \\(\\{3,4,\\dots ,n\\}\\) that are **not** the sums of the coordinates of any point already covered by the sunny lines.  \nFor each \\(c_j\\) let \\(L_{2+j}\\) be the line \\(x+y=c_j\\).  \nThese lines are non\u2011sunny (they are parallel to \\(x+y=0\\)) and are distinct because the constants \\(c_j\\) are distinct.\n\nBy the choice of the constants, every point of \\(S_n\\) that is not on \\(L_x\\) or \\(L_y\\) lies on one of the diagonals \\(D_{c_j}\\) or on a sunny line, hence is covered.\n\n---\n\n**Verification of the construction.**\n\n*Number of lines.*  \nWe have built exactly  \n\n\\[\n2\\;(\\text{border})\\;+\\;k\\;(\\text{sunny})\\;+\\;(n-2-k)\\;(\\text{diagonals})=n\n\\]\n\nlines.\n\n*Sunny count.*  \nOnly the \\(k\\) lines of Step\u202f2 are sunny, so the total number of sunny lines is precisely \\(k\\).\n\n*Coverage.*  \nEvery point of \\(S_n\\) belongs to one of the three families:\n\n* points with \\(a=1\\) or \\(b=1\\) lie on \\(L_y\\) or \\(L_x\\);\n* points belonging to a selected pair lie on the corresponding sunny line;\n* all remaining points have sum \\(a+b=c\\) with \\(c\\) equal to one of the chosen diagonal constants, hence lie on the corresponding diagonal line.\n\nThus the family \\(\\mathcal L\\) covers all points of \\(S_n\\).\n\n*Distinctness.*  \nThe two border lines are clearly distinct.  \nThe sunny lines are distinct because they are defined by disjoint pairs of points.  \nThe diagonal lines are distinct because they have different constants \\(c_j\\).  \nNo diagonal coincides with a border line because the borders have equations \\(y=1\\) or \\(x=1\\), which correspond to \\(c= a+1\\) with \\(a\\ge 2\\); the chosen constants are at least \\(3\\).  \nNo diagonal coincides with a sunny line because a sunny line is not parallel to \\(x+y=0\\).  \n\nHence all \\(n\\) lines are distinct.\n\nTherefore for every integer \\(k\\) with \\(0\\le k\\le n-2\\) a configuration satisfying the problem statement exists.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------\n#### 3.  Conclusion  \n\nFrom (1) we have the necessary condition \\(k\\le n-2\\).  \nThe explicit construction shows that this condition is also sufficient.  \n\nConsequently the set of all admissible values of \\(k\\) is  \n\n\\[\n\\boxed{\\{0,1,2,\\dots ,n-2\\}} .\n\\]"

### Summary  

**a. Verdict**  
I have not been able to produce a completely rigorous solution of the problem.  
What I can prove rigorously is the following partial result:

*For every integer \(n\ge 3\) the number \(k\) of sunny lines in any admissible configuration satisfies*
\[
0\;\le\;k\;\le\;n-2 .
\tag{∗}
\]

In particular, configurations with \(k=n-1\) or \(k=n\) are impossible.  
I have also exhibited a construction that works for every \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n-2\); however, the proof that the construction indeed covers all required points contains a gap (the argument that the remaining non‑sunny lines can always be chosen so that every uncovered point lies on one of them). Because of this gap I cannot claim that the set \(\{0,1,\dots ,n-2\}\) is the exact answer.

Thus the answer is **partially** resolved: the admissible values of \(k\) are contained in \(\{0,1,\dots ,n-2\}\), and I have a plausible construction for all those values, but a fully rigorous verification is still missing.

**b. Method Sketch**  

1. **Upper bound.**  
   * Count the total number of points that must be covered:
     \[
     |S_n|=\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\}
          =\binom{n+1}{2}.
     \]
   * Show that a sunny line can contain **at most two** points of \(S_n\) (otherwise three collinear lattice points would force the line to be parallel to one of the forbidden directions).  
   * Hence \(k\) sunny lines cover at most \(2k\) points.  
   * Any line that is parallel to the \(x\)-axis, the \(y\)-axis or the line \(x+y=0\) can contain at most \(n\) points of \(S_n\).  
   * Since there are exactly \(n\) lines in total, the remaining \(n-k\) (non‑sunny) lines can cover at most \(n(n-k)\) points.  
   * Therefore
     \[
     2k+n(n-k)\;\ge\;|S_n|=\frac{n(n+1)}{2},
     \]
     which simplifies to the inequality \((∗)\) above, i.e. \(k\le n-2\).

2. **Construction for the lower bound.**  
   * Take the two “border’’ lines \(y=1\) and \(x=1\); they are non‑sunny and together cover all points of \(S_n\) that lie on the two legs of the right‑angled triangle formed by \(S_n\).  
   * Choose \(k\) disjoint unordered pairs of interior points of \(S_n\) (points with both coordinates at least \(2\)). For each pair draw the unique line through it; by construction each of these lines is sunny and covers exactly the two points of its pair.  
   * The still‑uncovered points all have the same sum of coordinates, i.e. they lie on a diagonal of the form \(x+y=c\). Choose \(n-2-k\) distinct values of \(c\) (different from the sums already realised by the sunny lines) and draw the corresponding diagonal lines; each such line is parallel to \(x+y=0\) and therefore non‑sunny.  
   * The resulting family consists of exactly \(n\) distinct lines, contains precisely \(k\) sunny lines, and (the intended claim) covers every point of \(S_n\).

The upper‑bound argument is completely rigorous.  
The construction shows that the bound \(k\le n-2\) is at least attainable in principle, but a fully detailed verification that the chosen diagonals indeed cover **all** remaining points without creating overlaps or missing points has not been completed. Consequently the problem remains only partially solved.

**Summary**

**Final Verdict:** The solution is **invalid** because it contains a **Critical Error** that breaks the main argument; consequently the claimed upper bound \(k\le n-2\) is not justified. (In addition, the construction part contains a Justification Gap, but the critical error already invalidates the solution.)

**List of Findings**
* **Location:** “Show that a sunny line can contain **at most two** points of \(S_n\) (otherwise three collinear lattice points would force the line to be parallel to one of the forbidden directions).”
  * **Issue:** **Critical Error** – a sunny line can contain three (or more) points of \(S_n\); the argument that three points would force the line to be parallel to a forbidden direction is false (e.g. the line \(y=x\) contains \((1,1),(2,2),(3,3)\) for \(n\ge5\)). This invalidates the inequality used to obtain the upper bound \(k\le n-2\).
* **Location:** “The construction shows that the bound \(k\le n-2\) is at least attainable … the argument that the remaining non‑sunny lines can always be chosen so that every uncovered point lies on one of them.”
  * **Issue:** **Justification Gap** – the author does not rigorously prove that the chosen diagonal (non‑sunny) lines indeed cover all remaining points without overlap or omission. (This gap would need to be filled even if the upper bound were correct.)

---

**Detailed Verification Log**

Below the solution is reproduced in numbered parts; each part is examined in order.

---

### 1. Statement of the problem  

The problem is correctly restated. No issue.

---

### 2. “Partial result” claim  

> “For every integer \(n\ge 3\) the number \(k\) of sunny lines in any admissible configuration satisfies  
> \[
> 0\le k\le n-2 .
> \tag{∗}
> \]”

The author intends to prove (∗) by an upper‑bound argument and a construction. We verify each.

---

### 3. Upper‑bound argument  

#### 3.1 Counting the required points  

> “\( |S_n| = \#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\}= \binom{n+1}{2}\).”

*Correct.* The set \(S_n\) is the set of integer lattice points in the right‑angled triangle with vertices \((1,1),(n,1),(1,n)\). The number of such points is indeed \(\binom{n+1}{2}\).

#### 3.2 Claim about sunny lines  

> “Show that a sunny line can contain **at most two** points of \(S_n\) (otherwise three collinear lattice points would force the line to be parallel to one of the forbidden directions).”

**Critical Error.**  
A sunny line is defined only by *not* being parallel to the \(x\)-axis, the \(y\)-axis, or the line \(x+y=0\) (slope \(-1\)).  
Consider the line \(y=x\) (slope \(1\)). It is **not** parallel to any of the forbidden directions, hence it is sunny.  

For \(n\ge5\) the points  
\[
(1,1),\;(2,2),\;(3,3)\in S_n
\]
all lie on \(y=x\). Thus a sunny line can contain **three** points of \(S_n\).  
More generally, for any integer \(t\ge1\) the line \(y=x\) contains the points \((t,t)\) as long as \(2t\le n+1\); the number of such points is \(\left\lfloor\frac{n+1}{2}\right\rfloor\), which can be larger than \(2\).  

The reasoning “three collinear lattice points would force the line to be parallel to one of the forbidden directions’’ is false; three collinear lattice points can determine a line of any rational slope, not only the forbidden ones. Consequently the bound “each sunny line covers at most two points’’ is incorrect.

Because this claim is used to bound the total number of points covered by sunny lines (\(2k\)), the subsequent inequality is not justified.

#### 3.3 Claim about non‑sunny lines  

> “Any line that is parallel to the \(x\)-axis, the \(y\)-axis or the line \(x+y=0\) can contain at most \(n\) points of \(S_n\).”

*Correct.*  
- Horizontal line \(y=c\) (with \(1\le c\le n\)) contains points \((a,c)\) where \(1\le a\le n+1-c\); the maximum occurs at \(c=1\) giving \(n\) points.  
- Vertical line \(x=c\) is analogous.  
- Line \(x+y=c\) (parallel to \(x+y=0\)) contains the integer solutions of \(a+b=c\) with \(a,b\ge1\); there are \(c-1\) such solutions, and the largest possible \(c\) is \(n+1\), giving at most \(n\) points.

Thus this part of the argument is sound.

#### 3.4 Derivation of the inequality  

The author writes:

\[
2k + n(n-k) \ge |S_n| = \frac{n(n+1)}{2},
\]
which simplifies to \(k\le n-2\).

Because the term \(2k\) (the maximal contribution of sunny lines) is based on the false “at most two points per sunny line’’ claim, the inequality does **not** follow from the established facts. The derived bound \(k\le n-2\) is therefore unsupported. This is the **critical error** that invalidates the whole upper‑bound argument.

---

### 4. Construction for the lower bound  

The author proposes a construction that would achieve any \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n-2\). The steps are:

1. Use the two border lines \(y=1\) and \(x=1\) (non‑sunny).  
2. Choose \(k\) disjoint unordered pairs of interior points and draw the unique line through each pair; claim each such line is sunny and covers exactly its two points.  
3. The remaining uncovered points all lie on a diagonal \(x+y=c\); choose \(n-2-k\) distinct values of \(c\) and draw those diagonal lines (non‑sunny).

**Justification Gap.**  
The author does not prove rigorously that after steps 1 and 2 the set of uncovered points indeed consists *exactly* of points lying on a *single* diagonal \(x+y=c\) for each remaining point, nor that the chosen diagonals can be taken distinct and still cover all uncovered points without creating overlaps or leaving any point uncovered. A full verification would require:

* Showing that the interior pairs can be selected so that no two chosen sunny lines share a point and that the set of points left uncovered is a union of whole diagonals.  
* Proving that the number of needed diagonal lines is precisely \(n-2-k\) and that they can be chosen distinct from the sums already realized by the sunny lines.

Since this reasoning is omitted, the construction part contains a **Justification Gap**. Even if the gap were filled, the construction would only demonstrate the *attainability* of the bound; it would not rescue the invalid upper‑bound argument.

---

### 5. Overall assessment  

* The **upper‑bound** part, which is the core of the claimed result, relies on a false statement about sunny lines containing at most two points of \(S_n\). This is a **Critical Error** that breaks the logical chain and renders the inequality \(k\le n-2\) unjustified.  
* The **construction** part is incomplete; it has a **Justification Gap**, but this is secondary to the critical error above.

Because the solution’s main conclusion rests on the flawed upper‑bound argument, the solution cannot be considered correct. The author’s admission that the construction is not fully verified further confirms that the answer is not established. 

**Therefore, the solution is invalid.**

**Summary**

**a. Verdict**  
I have rigorously proved that for every integer \(n\ge 3\) the admissible values of \(k\) are exactly  

\[
\boxed{0\le k\le n } .
\]

For each \(k\) in this range a concrete configuration of \(n\) distinct lines satisfying the two conditions of the problem and containing exactly \(k\) sunny lines is exhibited; for any \(k>n\) such a configuration is impossible because at most \(n\) lines can be sunny.

**b. Method Sketch**  

1. **Notation.**  
   *A line is sunny* iff its slope is different from \(0\) (horizontal), \(\infty\) (vertical) and \(-1\) (the line \(x+y=0\)).  
   Let  

   \[
   S_n=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\}.
   \]

   The problem asks for \(n\) distinct lines covering every point of \(S_n\) and containing exactly \(k\) sunny lines.

2. **Key observation.**  
   For any integer \(t\) the line  

   \[
   \ell_t:\; y=x+t
   \]

   has slope \(1\) and therefore is sunny.  
   A point \((a,b)\in S_n\) satisfies \(b-a=t\) precisely when it lies on \(\ell_t\).

   The possible values of the difference \(b-a\) for points of \(S_n\) are the integers  

   \[
   0,1,\dots ,n-1 .
   \tag{1}
   \]

   Indeed, \(b-a\ge 0\) because \(a,b\ge 1\), and from \(a+b\le n+1\) we obtain  

   \[
   b-a\le (n+1-a)-a = n+1-2a\le n-1 .
   \]

3. **Construction for a given \(k\) (\(0\le k\le n\)).**  

   *Sunny part.*  
   Take the \(k\) sunny lines  

   \[
   \boxed{\ell_t\;(t=0,1,\dots ,k-1)}\qquad\text{ i.e. } y=x+t .
   \tag{2}
   \]

   They cover all points of \(S_n\) whose difference \(b-a\) belongs to \(\{0,1,\dots ,k-1\}\).

   *Non‑sunny part.*  
   For the remaining \(n-k\) lines we use the vertical lines  

   \[
   \boxed{x=j\;(j=1,2,\dots ,n-k)} .
   \tag{3}
   \]

   These lines are not sunny (slope \(\infty\)) and are pairwise distinct.

4. **Verification that every point of \(S_n\) is covered.**  

   Let \((a,b)\in S_n\) and put \(t=b-a\). By (1) we have \(0\le t\le n-1\).

   *Case 1: \(t\le k-1\).*  
   Then \((a,b)\) lies on the sunny line \(\ell_t\) from (2).

   *Case 2: \(t\ge k\).*  
   From the defining inequality \(a+b\le n+1\) we obtain  

   \[
   a+(a+t)\le n+1\;\Longrightarrow\;2a+t\le n+1\;\Longrightarrow\;
   a\le\frac{n+1-t}{2}\le\frac{n+1-k}{2}.
   \tag{4}
   \]

   When \(k\le n-1\) the right‑most inequality in (4) satisfies  

   \[
   \frac{n+1-k}{2}\le n-k,
   \tag{5}
   \]

   because it is equivalent to \(n+1-k\le 2n-2k\), i.e. \(k\le n-1\).  
   Hence \(a\) is an integer with \(1\le a\le n-k\), and the vertical line \(x=a\) (one of the lines in (3)) contains \((a,b)\).

   *Case 3: \(k=n\).*  
   In this situation there are no vertical lines; however (2) supplies the sunny lines \(\ell_t\) for **all** possible differences \(t\) (recall (1) gives \(t\le n-1\)). Thus every point of \(S_n\) belongs to some \(\ell_t\).

   Consequently every point of \(S_n\) lies on at least one of the \(n\) constructed lines for every admissible \(k\).

5. **Counting sunny lines.**  
   The lines in (2) have slope \(1\) and are therefore sunny; the lines in (3) are vertical and not sunny. Hence the total number of sunny lines in the configuration is exactly the chosen integer \(k\).

6. **Impossibility of larger \(k\).**  
   Because we are allowed only \(n\) distinct lines, the number of sunny lines cannot exceed \(n\). Therefore no configuration exists for \(k>n\).

   Combining the existence proved in steps 3–5 with this obvious upper bound yields the complete description of the admissible values of \(k\).

\[
\boxed{\text{All integers }k\text{ with }0\le k\le n\text{ are attainable, and no other }k\text{ works}.}
\]

**Final Verdict:** The solution is correct; it contains no critical errors or unjustified gaps.

**List of Findings:**
* No issues were identified; every step of the solution is rigorously justified.

---

### Detailed Verification Log

1. **Definition of sunny lines**  
   *Quote:* “A line is sunny iff its slope is different from 0 (horizontal), ∞ (vertical) and −1 (the line $x+y=0$).”  
   *Verification:* Correct definition matching the problem statement.

2. **Notation for the set of required points**  
   *Quote:* “\(S_n=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\mid a+b\le n+1\}\).”  
   *Verification:* Accurately captures all points \((a,b)\) with positive integers \(a,b\) and \(a+b\le n+1\).

3. **Observation about lines \(y=x+t\)**  
   *Quote:* “For any integer \(t\) the line \(\ell_t: y=x+t\) has slope 1 and therefore is sunny.”  
   *Verification:* Slope 1 is indeed different from 0, ∞, and −1, so the line is sunny.

4. **Range of the difference \(b-a\)**  
   *Quote:* “The possible values of the difference \(b-a\) for points of \(S_n\) are the integers \(0,1,\dots ,n-1\).”  
   *Justification:*  
   - Since \(a,b\ge1\), \(b-a\ge0\).  
   - From \(a+b\le n+1\) we have \(b-a\le (n+1-a)-a = n+1-2a\le n-1\) because \(a\ge1\).  
   Hence the claim is correct.

5. **Construction for a given \(k\) (0 ≤ k ≤ n)**  
   *Sunny part:* Lines \(\ell_t\) for \(t=0,1,\dots ,k-1\).  
   *Non‑sunny part:* Vertical lines \(x=j\) for \(j=1,2,\dots ,n-k\).  
   *Verification:*  
   - The \(k\) lines \(\ell_t\) are distinct (different intercepts).  
   - The \(n-k\) vertical lines are distinct (different \(x\)-coordinates).  
   - Sunny lines have slope 1; vertical lines have slope ∞, so exactly \(k\) of the \(n\) lines are sunny.

6. **Coverage of points with \(t\le k-1\)**  
   *Quote:* “If \(t\le k-1\) then \((a,b)\) lies on the sunny line \(\ell_t\).”  
   *Verification:* Directly follows from the definition of \(\ell_t\).

7. **Coverage of points with \(t\ge k\)**  
   *Derivation:*  
   \[
   a+(a+t)\le n+1\;\Longrightarrow\;2a+t\le n+1\;\Longrightarrow\;a\le\frac{n+1-t}{2}\le\frac{n+1-k}{2}.
   \]  
   *Quote:* “When \(k\le n-1\) the right‑most inequality satisfies \(\frac{n+1-k}{2}\le n-k\).”  
   *Verification:* Multiplying by 2 gives \(n+1-k\le2n-2k\), i.e. \(k\le n-1\), which holds under the stated condition. Hence \(a\) is an integer with \(1\le a\le n-k\), so the vertical line \(x=a\) (present in the construction) contains \((a,b)\).  

8. **Special case \(k=n\)**  
   *Quote:* “There are no vertical lines; however the sunny lines \(\ell_t\) for all \(t\) (0 ≤ t ≤ n‑1) cover every point.”  
   *Verification:* Since every point of \(S_n\) has a difference \(t\) in that range, it lies on one of the \(n\) sunny lines.

9. **Counting sunny lines**  
   *Quote:* “The lines in (2) have slope 1 and are therefore sunny; the lines in (3) are vertical and not sunny. Hence the total number of sunny lines is exactly \(k\).”  
   *Verification:* Straightforward counting; no overlap between the two families of lines.

10. **Impossibility of \(k>n\)**  
    *Quote:* “Because we are allowed only \(n\) distinct lines, the number of sunny lines cannot exceed \(n\).”  
    *Verification:* Trivial bound; a configuration with more than \(n\) sunny lines would require more than \(n\) distinct lines, contradicting the problem’s requirement.

11. **Conclusion**  
    *Quote:* “All integers \(k\) with \(0\le k\le n\) are attainable, and no other \(k\) works.”  
    *Verification:* The construction shows attainability for every \(k\) in the range, and the upper bound shows no larger \(k\) is possible. Hence the conclusion follows.

Since every logical step is justified and no gaps or errors are present, the solution fully resolves the problem.

Determine all nonnegative integers $k$ such that there exist $n$ distinct lines in the plane satisfying both the following:\n*   for all positive integers $a$ and $b$ with $a+b\\le n+1$, the point $(a,b)$ is on at least one of the lines; and\n*   exactly $k$ of the lines are sunny.\n\n======================================================================\n### Solution ###\n\nSummary**\n\n**a. Verdict**  \nI have rigorously proved that for every integer \\(n\\ge 3\\) the admissible values of \\(k\\) are exactly  \n\n\\[\n\\boxed{0\\le k\\le n } .\n\\]\n\nFor each \\(k\\) in this range a concrete configuration of \\(n\\) distinct lines satisfying the two conditions of the problem and containing exactly \\(k\\) sunny lines is exhibited; for any \\(k>n\\) such a configuration is impossible because at most \\(n\\) lines can be sunny.\n\n**b. Method Sketch**  \n\n1. **Notation.**  \n   *A line is sunny* iff its slope is different from \\(0\\) (horizontal), \\(\\infty\\) (vertical) and \\(-1\\) (the line \\(x+y=0\\)).  \n   Let  \n\n   \\[\n   S_n=\\{(a,b)\\in\\mathbb Z_{>0}^2\\mid a+b\\le n+1\\}.\n   \\]\n\n   The problem asks for \\(n\\) distinct lines covering every point of \\(S_n\\) and containing exactly \\(k\\) sunny lines.\n\n2. **Key observation.**  \n   For any integer \\(t\\) the line  \n\n   \\[\n   \\ell_t:\\; y=x+t\n   \\]\n\n   has slope \\(1\\) and therefore is sunny.  \n   A point \\((a,b)\\in S_n\\) satisfies \\(b-a=t\\) precisely when it lies on \\(\\ell_t\\).\n\n   The possible values of the difference \\(b-a\\) for points of \\(S_n\\) are the integers  \n\n   \\[\n   0,1,\\dots ,n-1 .\n   \\tag{1}\n   \\]\n\n   Indeed, \\(b-a\\ge 0\\) because \\(a,b\\ge 1\\), and from \\(a+b\\le n+1\\) we obtain  \n\n   \\[\n   b-a\\le (n+1-a)-a = n+1-2a\\le n-1 .\n   \\]\n\n3. **Construction for a given \\(k\\) (\\(0\\le k\\le n\\)).**  \n\n   *Sunny part.*  \n   Take the \\(k\\) sunny lines  \n\n   \\[\n   \\boxed{\\ell_t\\;(t=0,1,\\dots ,k-1)}\\qquad\\text{ i.e. } y=x+t .\n   \\tag{2}\n   \\]\n\n   They cover all points of \\(S_n\\) whose difference \\(b-a\\) belongs to \\(\\{0,1,\\dots ,k-1\\}\\).\n\n   *Non‑sunny part.*  \n   For the remaining \\(n-k\\) lines we use the vertical lines  \n\n   \\[\n   \\boxed{x=j\\;(j=1,2,\\dots ,n-k)} .\n   \\tag{3}\n   \\]\n\n   These lines are not sunny (slope \\(\\infty\\)) and are pairwise distinct.\n\n4. **Verification that every point of \\(S_n\\) is covered.**  \n\n   Let \\((a,b)\\in S_n\\) and put \\(t=b-a\\). By (1) we have \\(0\\le t\\le n-1\\).\n\n   *Case 1: \\(t\\le k-1\\).*  \n   Then \\((a,b)\\) lies on the sunny line \\(\\ell_t\\) from (2).\n\n   *Case 2: \\(t\\ge k\\).*  \n   From the defining inequality \\(a+b\\le n+1\\) we obtain  \n\n   \\[\n   a+(a+t)\\le n+1\\;\\Longrightarrow\\;2a+t\\le n+1\\;\\Longrightarrow\\;\n   a\\le\\frac{n+1-t}{2}\\le\\frac{n+1-k}{2}.\n   \\tag{4}\n   \\]\n\n   When \\(k\\le n-1\\) the right‑most inequality in (4) satisfies  \n\n   \\[\n   \\frac{n+1-k}{2}\\le n-k,\n   \\tag{5}\n   \\]\n\n   because it is equivalent to \\(n+1-k\\le 2n-2k\\), i.e. \\(k\\le n-1\\).  \n   Hence \\(a\\) is an integer with \\(1\\le a\\le n-k\\), and the vertical line \\(x=a\\) (one of the lines in (3)) contains \\((a,b)\\).\n\n   *Case 3: \\(k=n\\).*  \n   In this situation there are no vertical lines; however (2) supplies the sunny lines \\(\\ell_t\\) for **all** possible differences \\(t\\) (recall (1) gives \\(t\\le n-1\\)). Thus every point of \\(S_n\\) belongs to some \\(\\ell_t\\).\n\n   Consequently every point of \\(S_n\\) lies on at least one of the \\(n\\) constructed lines for every admissible \\(k\\).\n\n5. **Counting sunny lines.**  \n   The lines in (2) have slope \\(1\\) and are therefore sunny; the lines in (3) are vertical and not sunny. Hence the total number of sunny lines in the configuration is exactly the chosen integer \\(k\\).\n\n6. **Impossibility of larger \\(k\\).**  \n   Because we are allowed only \\(n\\) distinct lines, the number of sunny lines cannot exceed \\(n\\). Therefore no configuration exists for \\(k>n\\).\n\n   Combining the existence proved in steps 3–5 with this obvious upper bound yields the complete description of the admissible values of \\(k\\).\n\n\\[\n\\boxed{\\text{All integers }k\\text{ with }0\\le k\\le n\\text{ are attainable, and no other }k\\text{ works}.}\n\\]\n\n\n### Verification Task Reminder ###\n\nYour task is to act as an IMO grader. In your log, justify each correct step and explain in detail any errors or justification gaps you find, as specified in the instructions above.\n\n"
